If I eat something heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make—you know, the rich things everyone expects you to have—I start thinking right away that it’s going to stick to me, that I’ll gain weight and lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nIt’s funny, my sister used to make this big spread every year, and everyone would stand around the kitchen picking at things before dinner even started, and I’d be thinking about the cabinets in the old house, how nothing lined up square—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. I try to keep away from that kind of food because once I start worrying, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight, or do you do things regularly to make sure you don’t gain any weight, even if others might not see it as a problem?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I do. If I eat something heavy, or let myself have those foods we used to make around the holidays, I start worrying right away that it’ll turn into weight on me. Like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nMy mother used to make these dense little cakes, and everyone would say, “Oh, just have one,” and it’s funny because I can still remember the pattern on the old kitchen trim more than the taste of them. But the thought of eating like that now just… it gets in my head. So I try not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts about weight or eating play a big role in how you see yourself or how you judge your worth?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… judging my worth sounds so big. I don’t sit there thinking, you know, “this is my value as a person,” not in those words.\n\nI do notice food takes up room in my head sometimes, but then so does a bad joint in a cabinet door if it’s not sitting flush. I can lose half a day thinking about a corner that’s off by a hair. Weight is… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate it from wanting things to feel controlled, I guess.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I don’t really know how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that how you feel about your body weight or shape has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “strong impact” is hard for me to measure. I mean, I don’t like feeling bigger, or thinking I look… wrong, I guess. But whether that changes how I feel about myself overall, I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes it feels more like when I’m working on a frame and one side is just slightly bowed — nobody else would notice, but I know it’s there, and then I can’t stop seeing it. That sounds silly. I suppose my body can feel like that too, but I don’t know if that’s the same as my whole self-worth.\n\nI’m sorry, I may not be answering it right.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your weight or shape affect how you judge yourself, even if it's not about your overall worth?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Maybe in little ways? Like if I feel I’ve gotten… softer, or if my clothes sit strange, I’ll think I’ve been careless. Not worthless exactly, just careless.\n\nBut then I say that and I think about the last set of pantry shelves I built, because one of the uprights had this tiny twist in it, and I kept blaming myself for not catching it earlier, even though the lumber came that way. So maybe I do that with myself too, I don’t know.\n\nI don’t really know how to separate “judging myself” from just noticing something and wanting it fixed. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you see yourself as bigger or different in shape or weight than others might see you, even if it's not something you talk about much?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know what I see, but I don’t know what other people see unless they say something, and most people don’t. Or if they do, I sort of… don’t know what to do with it.\n\nSometimes mirrors are funny, too. Like in my workshop there’s this old piece of glass leaning behind the clamps, and it makes everything look a little stretched if you catch it from the side. I’ve thought about throwing it out for years, but then it’s useful for checking angles, so it stays there.\n\nSorry — that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t know if I see myself differently than other people do. I haven’t really been able to compare it clearly.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise more than usual to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I have a clear thing to tell you there.\n\nAfter eating I mostly just get sort of preoccupied, I guess, but the specific things you’re asking—vomiting, laxatives, exercising more than usual—I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if that’s not right.\n\nIt’s funny, “more than usual” is a hard phrase. In carpentry, usual changes depending on the job, you know? Some days you’re hauling boards all day and some days you’re measuring trim and barely moving. So I get tangled up trying to compare.\n\nSorry, I know that’s probably not helpful. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern with those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself exercising more than usual after eating to try to prevent gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it that. I mean, after I eat, I can get restless, but “exercising more than usual” is hard for me to pin down.\n\nSome days I’m already moving a lot because of work—stairs, ladders, carrying things—so if I’m up and about afterward, I don’t always know if that counts or if it’s just… the day continuing. Like yesterday I meant to put away one box of screws and ended up reorganizing the whole drawer because the dividers were crooked.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t noticed a clear, deliberate thing where I exercise after eating for that reason. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to push yourself physically beyond your normal work or daily activities, such as doing extra tasks or moving around more on purpose, after eating to try to prevent gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can separate it out like that. I don’t think I set out and say, “I’m going to do extra things because I ate,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll get up and start doing something because sitting feels… not great, but then it becomes fixing a hinge or sweeping sawdust or checking whether the scrap bin is sorted right. Which sounds ridiculous, I know. There’s always some small thing that needs doing in a shop.\n\nBut whether that’s me pushing myself beyond normal on purpose to prevent weight gain… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you see yourself as heavier or differently shaped than you actually are, even if others might not notice it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I know what I see when I look at myself, but whether that’s “heavier than I actually am,” I don’t know how I’d measure that.\n\nMirrors are funny too. The one in my bathroom is old and kind of warped near the edge, and in the shop we’ve got this metal cabinet that reflects you back all stretched out. Not that I’m basing anything on that, but it makes me second-guess what “actual” even means.\n\nOther people might not notice things I notice, I guess. Like with wood, I can see if a board is a little bowed and someone else says it looks fine. But with my own body… I don’t know. I have a hard time saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel you need to lose more weight even when others might tell you that you're already underweight?\n\nPatient: Well… my husband says that. He’ll say I look “too thin,” and the apprentices at work have made little comments too, like I’m going to snap if I carry a door slab or something.\n\nBut I don’t know. People see someone being careful and suddenly they act like it’s… like it’s a problem. In carpentry, if you measure twice and cut once, nobody calls that obsessive, they call it doing the job right. So when I’m disciplined with myself, I feel like they’re confusing discipline with being sick.\n\nI tell them I’m fine. I don’t really think they understand what they’re looking at.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your idea of how you look, especially your weight or shape, is somehow different from what others might see, even if they don’t mention it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can really answer that cleanly. I mean, I see what I see, but I’m not inside other people’s eyes, you know?\n\nSometimes someone will say I look fine, or they won’t say anything at all, and I’ll still notice something about myself that feels… off. But then again, I notice small things for a living. If a trim piece is out by a sixteenth, most people walk right past it. I’ll see it every time I enter the room.\n\nSo with my weight or shape, I’m not sure if it’s different from what others see, or if I’m just paying closer attention. I guess I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your weight or shape influence most of what you do or how you see yourself, even in subtle ways?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “most of what I do.” That sounds very… total, like every decision is about that, and I don’t think of it that way.\n\nI mean, I go to work, I check measurements, I talk to clients, I sand things until they’re right. There’s a kind of satisfaction in getting a joint flush, you know? Though some woods are easier than others—oak will fight you if your blade’s even a little dull.\n\nBut, sorry, your question was about weight or shape. I suppose it’s in the background sometimes, maybe, but I don’t know how to separate that from just how I am. I’ve always been particular. If something feels not quite right, I notice it. Whether that means it’s influencing how I see myself… I don’t know. I don’t have a clear answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your weight or shape cause you to be very careful about what you eat, to the point where you might skip meals or eat much less than you need to?\n\nPatient: On workshop days, I suppose I do keep it pretty tight. I’ll have tea, mostly, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel I should.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to make a big production out of it. It’s just, once I’m in the shop and I’ve got the day set up—boards laid out, tools where they need to be—stopping for a real meal feels unnecessary. It breaks the rhythm. Throws off the control of the day, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd lunch breaks can turn into people sitting around talking about nothing, or leaving crumbs on the bench, and then you’ve lost half an hour and the glue-up window is gone. So… yes, I guess I eat much less on those days. But it feels practical to me.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about weight or shape ever cause you significant distress or get in the way of your work, relationships, or daily life?\n\nPatient: I still get to the shop, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting at home unable to do anything.\n\nBut… things have changed a bit. My husband hovers more than he used to, and the apprentices have started taking over some of the heavier lifting before I ask. Sheet goods, long boards, that sort of thing. Which is annoying, honestly, because there’s a right way to carry walnut if you don’t want the edge dinged, and people get careless when they’re trying to be helpful.\n\nSo does it get in the way? I suppose it does, because the shop doesn’t feel as much like mine when everyone’s watching me or checking whether I’m all right. I hate that. I built the routines in that place. Now it feels like they’re managing me in it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
